DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Sir:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
1.
We have been advised of a memorandum issued by SCAP
in Japan under date of April 22, 1948 concerning the restoration
of property owned by nationals of the United Nations which was
located in Japan prior to the outbreak of the war and which has been
the subject of confiscation, dispossession, spoilation and other
measures taken on the part of the Japanese Government or Japanese.
2.
We assume that these provisions include rights of
industrial property, such as patents and trade-marks which were re-
corded in the Patent Office in the name of American nationals before
the war and which have been acted against by the Japanese Government.
3.
In this connection, we understand that the so-called
War-Time Law of Industrial Property was promulgated early in 1942
by Japan under which the Patent Office proceeded to cancel all patents
and trade-marks of record in the name of American nationals.
4.
We respectfully submit that, instead of requiring
American nationals, owmers of such rights of industrial property
in Japan, to apply for restoration of their property and then wait
upon action of the Japanese Government for the restoration of such
rights, it would be more equitable, expedient and simple if the
CS/V
1948
OPASE SAE
Japanese Government should pass a law repealing the so-called War-
Time Law of Industrial Property of 1942 as to all its effects, and
OFFIE OF
providing that all patents and trade-marks in the name of American
12
nationals and also nationals of the United Nations be deemed to have
never been cancelled and to be in full force and effect as if such
D
Law of 1942 had never been passed. If such a new law is passed and
15
communicated to the Patent Office, the said patents and trade-marks
would be deemed to remain on the records of the Patent Office in Japan.
The Office will be required to remove from the records the entry of
cancellation of American trade-marks and patents, thereby restoring
the pre-war situation.
6
-5.
It is, of course, evident that there should also be
smaderprovision in due course to the effect that the period as between
the outbreak of the war and the restoration of normal relations between
m

any rights that may have lapsed in the meantime by reason of non-renewal
of registrations, expiration of legal periods for opposition or cancella-
tion, payment of annuities, etc. may be completely restored.
Respectfully
LANGNER PARRY CARD & LANGNER

My dear Mr. Ledas:
I refer to your letter of September 9, 1948, in
which you raise gueries with respect to a SCAP memorandum
of April 22, 1948, dealing with restoration of property
in Janan to United Nations nationals. The memorandum
to which you refer is not concerned with the problem of
the restoration of industrial nronerty rights, which,
894.542/9-948
because of its comnlexity, is being handled separately
in other ocuments.
As I informed you orally last weelk, a program has
been worked out for the restoration in Japan of industrial
oronerty rights of foreign nationals, and this program
is now before the Far Eastern Comaission for its approval.
Although considerable time is involved in obtaining this
annroval, it appears to the Department to be highly
desirable th: t this sten be taken prior to action by SCAP,
in view of the equally valid interest of other nations
in the progre. It is particularly importent in view of
the fact that substantiel agreement among interested
countries may now forestall a later reopening of the
issue in connection with the drafting of a peace treaty
with Janan. e expect that SCAP will be ble to implement
this program before the end of the year.
7
The program, with respect to patents, as presently
constituted, calis for the revalidation and restoration of
all foreign-owned rights vhich were cequestered during the
war or which lansed for non-vayment of fees. It provides

With respect to trade-marks, the program is less well

We fully recognize the a dvantages which would be

by revocation of the wartime Law of Industrial Property.
However, it will probably be necessary for a number of

I have the honor to refer to this : ission's despatch No. 534
of August 12, 1943.entitled mransmittal of SCAP Memorandum for the
Japanese Governnent concerning Trademarks, etc., dated July 29,
1948".
It wi 1l le rocalled that ScAPIN 1923, which was enclosed with
that despatch, required the Japanese Government to enact legislation
providing that exclusive use of certain holding company and monopoly
ACTION
tralemarks by subsidiaries or successor companies to the original
users co ld be prohibited at the discretion of the Holding Company
00P1ES
Liquidation Commission, and that free use of such trademarks by any
TO:
and all manufacturers should be allowed in the future.
It is now reported by officials in the Anti-Trust and Cartels
Division of the Economic and Scientific Section of this Headquarters
that the implementing of SCAPIN 1923 has been suspended pending fur-
ther study and determination of policy, apparently on the basis of a
complaint received by the Deconcentration Review Foard from the
Westinghouse Electric Jompany.
The Westinghouse complaint dwelt in general terms on the ques-
tion of the reorganization of the Mitsubishi Electric Company, in
which Westinghouse was before the war a minor stockholder, and
recommended strongly that Mitsuhishi Electric be left as one opera
ting unit, considerable diversity leing required in the type of pro
duction in which !itsubishi and .estinghouse are jointly engaged.
The Anti-Trust and Cartels Division states that, though plans for
organization of litsubishi Electric have not been completed, the
company will probahly still be larger after reorganization than it
as before the war.
With regarl to the SCAPIN concerning trademarks, Westinghouse
Contidential
pointed out that consideralle injustice would result in the case of
 company, specifically the itsubishi Pencil Company, which used
the Mitsubishi trademark without being a part of the combine. Though
the Anti-Trust and Cartels Division insists that a careful reading of
the SCAPIN

cognizance of a case only after the designated company has received
a preliminary order for reorganization. In the case of Mitsubishi
Electric, no such order, so far as this Mission is aware, has been
dispatched.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 627
dated September 23, 1948 from the Office
of the United States Political Adviser
at Tokyo, on the subject of: "Japanese
Registration of Foreign Patents."
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
1330
Press Release
14 September 1948.
TO ACCEPT FOREIGN APPLICATIONS FOR PATENT RIGHTS
Foreign applications for the registration of patent property
rights may now be accepted by the Japanese Patent Office and
processed under the Japanese law, it was announced today by SCAP's
Civil Property Custodian.
Heretofore, the acceptance of applications for patent rights
from persons outside of Japan has been prohibited. A SCAP direc-
tive issued today to the Japanese Government removes all restric-
tions on the filing of such applications and permits the Board of
Patents and Standards to register patents, utility models, and
designs in the names of foreign applicants under the provisions of
the Japanese law and the International Convention for the Protec-
tion of Industrial Property. At the same time, a change is being
made in the censorship regulations to permit sending of patent
specifications through the mails. This will permit foreign appli-
cants to send descriptions of their inventions into Japan in con-
nection with their applications for property protection under the
law.
Col. E. C. Miller, Deputy Civil Property Custodian, today pointed
out that, as a result of the new directive, a foreign applicant for
patent property registration in Japan will get the benefits of the
priority provisions of the International Convention. These provide,
in effect, that an applicant for a patent who has filed application
in another member country within twelve months preceding shall have
priority rights over a Japanese user of the invention who may have
filed application during that period. Since most of the leading
industrial countries are members of the International Convention,
this means that inventors in those countries may obtain the protec-
tion of a Japanese patent on inventions filed for registration else-
where within the past twelve months.
As a result of this important forward step in property adminis-
tration, recently patented inventions may now be introduced into
Japan, Colonel Miller pointed out, to the benefit of the nation's
economy and with full protection to the foreign inventor. It is
axpected that many such inventors will seek this protection now in
order that they may later enter into licensing agreements with
Japaneso manufacturers.
The Deputy Civil Property Custodian, however, stated that under
existing Japanese law no priority rights can be granted to appli-
cants who filed in their home countries more than twelve months
prior to filing in Japan, nor can such applicants be granted reg-
istration under this law if a foreign patent was issued and
published

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's 627,
September 23, 1948.
- 2 -
published more than 12 months prior to application in Japan. No
change in these rules has been made by SCAP's new patent property
directive. Officials of the Japanese Patent Office expressed
their enthusiastic approval of SCAP's action and announced their
intention to resume exchange of official publications with the
patent offices of other countries throughout the world.

Attention: Commercial Division
Dear Sirs:
We have been informed that it is now possible to apply for pat-
ents in Japan and would appreciate your having this fact confirmed.
We have also been informed that the Deputy Civil Property Cus-
todian has stated that under existing Japanese laws no priorty rights can
be granted to applicants who filed in their home country more than twelve
months prior to filing in Japan. Additionally, applicants cannot be granted
registration of a patent if a foreign patent was issued and published more
than twelve prior to the application in Japan. If this is true, it would
place American business interest in Japan in a position to sustain consid-
erable loss on inventions made during the war years. In view of this, we
would greatly appreciate any information that you can give us concerning
patents and patent rights in Japan atthe present time and also the proper
channels to be followed in applying for Japanese patent protection.
Very truly yours,
AMERICAN CHEMICAL PAINT COMPANY

Subject: The Industrial Property
Situation in Japan
814
Dear Mr. Saltzman:
894.542/10-1348
Subsequent to your letter of September 20, representatives
of the Council met with representatives of the Department of State in
xx
Washington on September 24. The Council delegation headed by W. E. F.
Bradley, Chairman of the Industrial Property Committee, NFTC, included
Messrs. A. T. Brown of the Japan Committee, NFTC, and E. L. Behr, Jr.,
NFTC. Representatives of the Department were Messrs. Geoffrey W. Lewis,
890
Roger C. Dixon and R. Burr Smith. There is attached a statement con-
firming the recommendations presented verbally at the meeting.
Our representatives were given to understand that the Far
East Commission is now actively considering the question of Japanese
industrial property and the impression was gained that any action beyond
that authorized by SCAP's directive of September 7 may not be expected
pending the decisions of the Far East Commission and the issuance by the
Commission of a directive to SCAP.
The directive of September 7 meets only one of the recom- 
mendations advanced by our Committee, namely, that patent applications of
foreign nationals are now to be accepted and processed. In the judgment
S
of our Committee the other recommendations could be carried out by SCAP
without the delay which we fear will result through action to reach unani-
mous agreement within the Commission. It is the opinion of our Committee
B
that its recommendations are fair and reasonable to the nationals of all
member nations of the Far East Commission and, therefore, present no
ground for dissent within the Commission. We are not informed as to what
other issues relating to patents and trademarks are on the agenda of the
Commission and it is possible that subjectsof a controversial nature may
894.542/10-1348
postpone for some months any further clarification in the Japanese
situation. If such be the case we suggest that, in the interest of the
United States economic policy toward Japan, SCAP be authorized to act
along the line of our recommendations to clarify the Japanese industrial
property situation. This is one of the major considerations associated
with the return of American industry to its prewar status in Japan.
We will be very glad to have your reaction to this proposal.
NOV 12 1948
INTERNATIONAL RES
Sincerely yours,
FILED
00T
Robert F. Loree
DEPARTMENT OF SIATE
Chairman of the Board

Coble Address. NAPTRAC
Ostober 7, 1948
Ref. No. M-1014
JAPANESE PATENTS. TRADEMARKS AND MARKS OF ORIGIN
Recommendations submitted to Representatives of the Depertment of
State by Representatives of the Industrial Property Committee. HFTC,
At a Meeting in Washington on September 24. 1948
Patents
The following recommendations were made in connection with patents:
1. That the Japanese Patent Office be reestablished to accept and process
applications from foreigners as well as from Japanese nationals.
2. That the term of all patents in force on December 7, 194l be extended
for a period of approximately 7 years.
3. That priority rights in effect December 7, 194l be extended for 7 years
plus an additional period of approximately 9 months.
4. That all patents cancelled by Japanese war legislation be reinstated.
5. That all taxes for the period December 7, 194l to December 1, 1948 be
waived.
6. That no compulsory licensing or any other plan for diminishing the value
of the right "to exclude" be enacted.
7. That foreigners have restored to them the right to enter into licensing
agreements and technical service agreements with Japanese nationals or
to resume or modify agreements existing on December 7, 1941.
8. On the assumption that there is no intent to carry out any fundamental
changes in the Japanese Patent Law, that the existing law be modified in
the following respects:
a. The unusually severe penalties should be made less stringent.
b. Provisions for opposition proceeding, tending to encourage
litigation, should be eliminated.
c. The requirement for payment of annual taxes, which frequently
imposes hardships on investors of limited resources, should
be eliminated.
d. The provisions for working patents should be eliminated from

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
the law. In addition to the general trend away from such
requirements, the practical reason for their deletion may
be found in the time lag between invention and production
on any given product.
Country of Origin Markings on Goods Exported from Japan
On February 20, 1947 SCAP issued instructions to the Japanese govern-
ment as follows:
1. The Imperial Japanese Government is hereby directed to take
immediate steps to insure that every article prepared for ex-
port after 15 days of receipt of this Directive, the imediate
container thereof and the outside package will be marked, stamped,
branded or labeled in legible English with the words "Made in
Occupied Japan."
2. All marking, stamping, branding or labeling shall be made in a
conspicuous place and shall be as nearly indelible and permanent
as the nature of the Article will permit.
In view of informal reports from Japan that there is an apparent loop-
hole which permits a foreign buyer to request the omission of country of origin
markings, the Committee recommended strict enforcement of the above instructions.
Access to Japanese Scientific and Technical Information in Japan
The Committee expressed its disapproval of the principle as well as
the procedure in carrying out the Far East Commission directive permitting
technical representatives of member governments of the Far East Commission to
take copies of technical and scientific processes which are of Japanese origin
and ownership and which were developed prior to December 31, 1945. The Committee
argued that the principle involved is highly inconsistent with the announced
policy to restore Japan to a self-supporting basis. The committee also pointed
out that the beneficial interests of United States nationals in Japanese techniques
and patents as well as exchange of scientific information agreements are in jeopardy.

TO MEMBERS OF
:NAFTRAC
NATIONAL FOREIGN TRADE COUNCIL,
111 BROADWAY · NEW YORK 6, N. Y.
October 4, 1948
JAPANESE PATENTS, TRADEMARKS AND MARKS OF ORIGIN
Progress Report By Industrial Property Committee, NFTC
Background of Committee Action
Early in 1948 the NFTC Industrial Property Committee began discussions with the
Department of State with the object of clarifying the patent and trademark situation in
Japan. In addition to seeking protection of United States interests, the Committee urged
early resumption of the acceptance and processing of patent applications of foreign nation-
als, a privilege denied by the Japanese War Time Law on Industrial Property. (See Attach-
ment A)
In the course of a conference with the Under Secretary of the Army and the Assis-
tant Secretary of State for Occupied Areas, late in July, arrangements were made for further
detailed discussion and presentation of the Committee's views.
Recent Developments
On August 3l recommendations of the Committee were submitted formally to the De-
partment of State and on September 24 representatives of the Committee met in Washington
with the Officers of the State Department and discussed these recommendations which were
as follows:
1. That the Japanese Patent Office be reestablished to accept and process
applications from foreigners as well as from Japanese nationals.
2. That the terms of all patents in force on December 7, 1941 be extended
for a period of approximately 7 years.
3. That priority rights in effect December 7, 1941 be extended for 7 years
plus an additional period of approximately 9 months.
4. That all patents cancelled by Japanese war legislation be reinstated.
5. That all taxes for the period December 7, 1941 to December 1, 1948 be
waived.
6.
That no compulsory licensing or any other plan for diminishing the value
of the right "to exclude."
7. That foreigners have restored to them the right to enter into licensing
agreements and technical service agreements with Japanese nationals or
to resume or modify agreements existing on December 7, l941.
While it is understood that there is no intention in government circles
to carry out any fundamental changes in the Japanese Patent Law, the
Committee has recommended that the law be modified in the following
respects:
480
NOTE: This bulletin to members is not for publication or redistribution.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
a. The unusually severe penalties should be made less stringent.
b.
Provisions for opposition proceeding, tending to encourage
litigation, should be eliminated.
C.
The requirement for payment of annual taxes, which frequently
imposes hardships on investors of limited resources, should
be eliminated.
a.
The provisions for working patents should be eliminated from
the law. In addition to the general trend away from such
requirements, the practical reason for their deletion may
be found in the time lag between invention and production
on any given product.
The day before the September 24 meeting word reached the Committee
from J pan that SCAP (Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers) had issued a
directive to the Japanese government on September 7 changing the Japanese
patent and trademark situation. Copy of this directive has since been re-
ceived and is attached hereto. (See Attachment B). Copy of SCAP press release
of September 14 is also attached. (See Attachment C).
Future Program
The SCAP directive of September 7, while representing some progress,
does not meet all of the conditions set forth in the eight-point program out-
lined above end it is the intention of the Committee to continue its negoti-
ations with the Tepartment of State in the hopes of achieving its aims. The
Committee's opinion is that the action it has recommended does not present
serioue complications nor ground for dissent within the Far East Commission
and could be carried out in a relatively simple manner. However, it is under-
stood that the whole problem of the rights of foreigners in Japanese patents
and trademarks, including highly technical considerstions with respect to in-
fringements, misuse, claims, etc. is now being discussed in the Fer East Com-
mission and for that reason the Committee gained the impression that no further
action in broadening or extending SCAP directive of September 7 may be expected
pending a directive from the Far East Commission several months hence. It is
the opinion of the Committee that this delay is rather serious and an effort
will be made to bring about direct action on the part of SCAP.
Other Matters Before the Committee
Country of Origin Markings on Goods Exported from Japan
On February 20, 1947 SCAP issued instructions to the Japanese govern-
ment as follows:
The Imperial Japanese Government is hereby directed to take
immediate steps to insure that every article prepared for ex-
port after 15 days of receipt of this Directive, the immediate
container thereof and the outside package will be marked, stamped,
branded or labeled in legible English with the words "Made in
Occupied Japan."
2.
All marking, stamping, branding or labeling shall be made in a
conspicuous place and shall be as nearly indelible and permanent as
the nature of the Article will permit.

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
In view of informal reports from Japan that there is an apparent loop-
hole which permits a foreign buyer to request the omission of country of origin
markings, the Committee has taken this up with the Department of State urging
strict enforcement of the above instructions and the State Department has agreed
to investigate the situation.
Access to Japanese Scientific and Technical Information in Japan
The Committee has expressed its disapproval of the principle as well as
the procedure in carrying out the Far East Commission directive permitting tech-
nical representatives of member governments of the Far East Commission to take
copies of technical and scientific processes which are of Japanese origin and
ownership and which were developed prior to December 31, 1945. The Committee has
argued that the principle involved is highly inconsistent with the announced
policy to restore Japan to a self-supporting basis and that the beneficial in-
terests of United States nationals in Japanese techniques and patents as well
as exchange of scientific information agreements are in jeopardy.

(Unofficial Translation)
Attachment A to Bulletin No. 1543
THE WARTIME LAW OF INDUSTRIAL PROPERTY
(Law No. 21 of Year 1917)
Article I. Patents or registration of industrial properties applied for
or requested by an enemy person shall be suspended during the war-time.
In case an invention, model or design under application or
requisition as provided for in the previous paragraph comes to fall under
any one of the following cases, no patent or registration shall be per-
mitted for such application or requisition:
1. Known openly or used openly in the domain of the Japanese Empire:
2. Described to such an extent as to be easily applied in a publication
circulated in the domain of the Japanese Empire.
Article 2. No enemy person may claim for a hearing or a complaint hearing, or
bring a suit against a complaint.
Article 3. No enemy person may claim the priority right as provided for the
Article 4 of the International Convention for Protection of Industrial
Properties for the right of patent originated during the war-time.
Article 4. The patent or the registration of trade mark belonging to an
enemy person may be cancelled according to the provisions of the or-
dinances, in case it is required on account of the military or public
necessity due to the situation.
Article 5. A person, who has been so licensed, may use exclusively a patent
or an invention which belongs to an enemy person. This applies also to
such patents or inventions which have been cancelled in accordance with
the provision of the preceding article.
The duration of the right of exclusive use as provided for in the
preceding paragraph shall be for the period fixed within the remaining
period of the right of patent concerned.
Necessary matters pertaining to the right of exclusive use shall
be provided for by an Imperial ordinance.
Article 6. No trade mark, which is the same with or similar to the trade mark
the registration of which has been cancelled according to the provisions
of Article 4 and is to be used for the same commodity, shall be registered.
Article 7. Provisions in the preceding six articles shall apply with necessary
modifications to an application, a requisition, a suit, a right of patent
or a right of trade mark of a person other than that of an enemy, should
the interests thereof be enjoyed mainly by an enemy person.
Article 8. Matters necessary at the termination of the war shall be provided
for by an Imperial ordinance.
Article 9. Any person who has tresspassed on the right of exclusive use under
this law shall be punished with penal servitude not longer than five years
or with a fine not more than one thousand yen.
----End----

=== Page 28 === (confidence 1.00)
Attachment C to Bulletin 1543
Statement issued September 14, 1948
By Public Relations Office, G.H.Q. Toyko
Applications Accepted Here For Foreign Patent Rights
Foreign applications for the registration of patent property rights may
now be accepted by the Japanese Patent Office and processed under the Jap-
anese law, it was announced yesterday by SCAP's Civil Property Custodian.
Heretofore, the acceptance of applications for patent rights from per-
sons outside of Japan has been prohibited. A SCAP directive issued Tuesday
to the Japanese Government removes all restrictions on the filing of such
applications and permits the Board of Patents and Standards to register
patents, utility models and designs in the names of foreign applicants under
the provisions of the Japanese law and the International Convention for the
Protection of Industrial Property. At the same time a change is being made
in the censorship regulations to permit sending of patent specification:
through the mails. This will permit foreign applicants to send descriptions
of their inventions into Japan in connection with their applications for
property protection under the law.
Col. E. C. Miller, Deputy Civil property custodian, pointed out that,
as a result of the new directive, a foreign applicant for patent property
registration in Japan will get the benefits of the priority provisions of
the International Convention. These provide, in effect, that an applicant
for a patent who has filed application in another member country within 12
months preceding shall have priority rights over a Japanese user of the in-
vention who may have filed application during the period. Since most of the
leading industrial countries are members of the International Convention, this
means that inventors in those countries may obtain the protection of a Jap-
anese patent on inventions filed for registration elsewhere within the past
12months.
As a result of this important forward step in property administration,
recently patented inventions may now be introduced into Japan, Colonel Miller
pointed out, to the benefit of the nation's economy and with full protection
to the foreign inventor. It is expected that many such inventors will seek
this protection now in order that they may later enter into licensing agree-
ments with Japanese manufacturers.
The Deputy Civil Property Custodian, however, stated that under existing
Japanese law no priority rights can be granted to applicants who filed in
their home countries more than 12 months prior to filing in Japan, nor can
such applicants be granted registration under this law if a foreign patent
was issued and published more than 12 months prior to application in Japan.
No change in these rules han been made by SCAp's new patent property dir-
ective. Officials of the Japanese Patent Office expressed their enthusiastic
approval of SCAP's action and announced their intention to resume exchange
of official publications with the patent offices of other countries through-
out the world.

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
NFTC Ref. No. M-1215
December 28, 1948
NFTC Memorandum
on
Comments by SCAP
Relative to Japanese Patents
A conference attended by members of the staff of SCAP and others,
Brigadier General J. W. O'Brien, U.S.A., presiding, was held in Tokyo on
October 19, 1948 to consider recommendations concerning Japanese patents, trade
marks, and marks of origin, set forth in NFTC Bulletin No. 1543, a copy of which
is attached.
Certain points were raised by SCAP representatives on which NFTC has
been requested to comment further. Following are the points raised by SCAP, and
NFTC replies to these questions and comments.
SCAP'S COMMENT CONCERNING RECOMMENDATION #2
In regard to Recommendation #2 under "Recent Development" of the Bulle-
tin, it was stated that some of the patents in force on 7 December l941 had been
maintained in force during the war. It was explained that some Japanese patents
owned by foreign nationals had been cancelled, some had lapsed due to non-pay-
ment of fees, etc., but there were cases where annual fees had either been paid
in lump sum before the war or had been paid by Japanese attorneys and others dur-
ing the war. SCAP representative asked if the recommendation was intended to in-
clude the still existing patents.
NFTC'S COMMENT:
Yes, the recommendation is intended to include still existing patents.
A period from December 7, 194l until the present time, (or approximately 7 years)
should be excluded from the normal term of rights in industrial property which
were in force in Japan on December 7, 1941, or which are recognized or establish-
ed under Recommendation #3 of NFTC Bulletin 1543 and belong to any of the allied
or associated powers or their nationals. The normal duration of such rights shall
be deemed to be automatically extended in Japan for a further term of 7 years.
The term "belong to" as used above, includes patents in which allied or associated
powers or their nationals had a legal, equitable, or reversionary interest.
SCAP'S COMMENTS CONCERNING RECOMMENDATION #6
With regard to Recommendation #6, it was asked if it were intended to
oppose the introduction of any extension of the existing compulsory licensing
system in Japan or whether it was intended that this existing system should be
eliminated. A SCAP representative explained that many countries have compulsory
licensing provisions similar to those which appear in Japanese law and that it

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
was assumed that the recommendation related to any extension beyond this system,
such as providing for mandatory non-exclusive licensing.
NFTC'S COMMENT:
Provisions for compulsory licensing of a patent, if the patent is not
worked within three years after grant, appear in the patent laws of several for-
eign countries. Results in these countries show that very few compulsory licen-
ses have been granted. Any form of compulsory licensing is believed undesirable
particularly for individual inventors or small manufacturers. For example, an in-
ventor attempting to negotiate the sale of an important invention which he is un-
able to finance conceivably can be forced to grant a license on terms established
by the government.
******
Several points were raised concerning the modification of Japanese
Patent Law as set forth in Recommendation #8 a, b, c, and d. NFTC representatives
have emphasized, in conference with representatives of the Departments of State,
and the Army, that these recommendations were made in the hope that Japanese
Patent Law might be modified to conform with the U. S. patent system, which is
believed to be the most satisfactory system yet developed.
SCAP'S COMMENT CONCERNING RECOMMENDATION #8a.
In regard to Recommendation #8a, this seemed to SCAP representatives to
be rather surprising. It had been assumed that American manufacturers would de-
sire even more stringent penalties as a means of discouraging copying of the in-
ventions which they introduced into Japan and patented here.
NFTC'S COMMENT:
The criminal proceedings carrying penalties of fine and imprisonment
seem unnecessary and undesirable in patent law. Recovery of damages, and the se-
curing of injunctions are adequate remedies for breaches of law relating to in-
dustrial property.
a) The severe penalties, including imprisonment for a term of 5 years,
are hardly warranted under present status of world-wide technological progress.
Frequently, an infringer whose technical knowledge is limited, an experience pri-
marily commercial, may not be aware of an adversely held patent. Even an exten-
sive patent search would not insure immunity since it is well know among patent
experts that final determination of whether or not a patent is infringed fre-
quently will depend on the final determination of a court.
SCAP'S COMMENT CONCERNING RECOMMENDATION #8b.
In regard to #8b, a SCAP representative suggested that, if opposition
proceedings are eliminated, it would necessitate Japan following the American
practice of making very thorough novelty examination prior to registration. He
said that there is no doubt in his mind that the American practice is very bene-
ficial but at present the Japanese Patent Office neither has the facilities nor
could it afford to follow this practice. If opposition proceedings were elimin-
ated, there would be even less surety than at present that a Japanese patent

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
would withstand attack upon its validity. The spokesman said he did not under-
stand the contention that these proceedings tended to encourage litigation. He
suggested that, in the absence of such proceedings, the more complex and expensive
litigation before the courts would have to be resorted to to replace the present
provision whereby opponents could contest granting of a patent prior to registra-
tion. At present, these cases are heard initially by the Patent Offices itself.
NFTC'S COMMENT:
Prior to the war, the Japanese Patent Office conducted complete, thorough
and efficient, novelty examinations. This procedure should be resumed as soon as
possible and opposition proceedings eliminated from the law. Publication of appli-
cations would be unnecessary except for the purpose of opposition so that with no
provision for opposition proceedings, this expense is avoided. As to the opposi-
tion proceedings themselves, they may readily become extensive, protracted and the
subject of unscrupulous practices. For example, in a number of countries it has
been usual for certain concerns to oppose practically every application in their
line of business, and to obtain free licenses in return for their withdrawal from
the opposition. Under opposition proceedings as opposed to the United States system
of examination for novelty, there is a considerable tendency for examiners to base
their judgment on the recommendation of interested persons rather than making in-
dependent searches and decisions.
The term "litigation" as used in NFTC Recommendation #8b was meant to
include litigation in the Patent Office as well as in the courts.
SCAP'S COMMENT CONCERNING RECOMMENDATION #8C.
In regard to Recommendation #8c, it was said that if the annual taxes are
eliminated, it would mean that the initial application and registration fees would
have to be raised very considerably to compensate the loss of revenue. This would
have the effect of discouraging inventors of limited resources from applying for
patents in the first instance. The annual taxes are designed to assist such in-
ventors. A SCAP representative pointed out that they could be regarded as pay-
ment, on an installment system, of those fees necessary to maintain the patent of-
fice. There is no necessity for a patentee to continue payment of these taxes
if his invention is not a success whereas, if the invention is put to use, the
payment of the fees do not entail any great hardship. Taxes also have the benefit
of helping to maintain a pure register of patents.
NFTC'S COMMENT:
The requirement for the payment of annual fees is considered undesirable.
Generally, the amount of revenue produced is comparatively small, and such a tax
works to the disadvantage of the poor inventor and manufacturer.
The maintenance of a "pure register" of patents does not seem to be suf-
ficiently important to compensate for the harm which may result to a patentee who
through lack of funds allows a patent to lapse. In any event, it is difficult
to determine in what respect the payment of annual taxes helps to maintain a
"Pure Register" of patents. Patents receive consecutive numbers upon issuance,
and it would seem that such a register can be more easily checked than one from
which patent numbers are continuously removed or cancelled as soon as a yearly
tax remains unpaid.

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Reference was made by SCAP representative to the necessity for such
fees for the maintenance of the Patent Office. It may be pointed out that the
United States Patent Office is not self-sustaining and there would seem to be no.
particular reason for requiring the Patent Office of Japan to be self-sustaining.
SCAP'S COMMENT CONCERNING RECOMMENDATION #8d.
With regard to Recommendation #8d, a SCAP spokesman said that he be-
lieved that the Japanese law provided that penalty for non-working would not be
exercised in the event that a patentee could prove he had made serious attempts
to work the invention but had been barred from doing so for good reason. He said
that he would investigate this matter further to insure that is the case. If it
is, it would be an adequate defense to an application for compulsory license,
based on the working requirements, that there was, in fact, a time lag existing
between invention and production on any given product.
NFTC'S COMMENT:
At the present, there is a world-wide trend toward the elimination of
working provisions. The London Conference of l934, adopted the provision that the
non-working of a patent should not be a cause for cancellation during the first
3 years of registration. It is expected that complete elimination of working pro-
visions at the next international convention will be advocated. Frequently, im-
portant inventions require a considerable period of time to be developed commer-
cially; the average being about 8 years.
MARK OF ORIGIN MARKINGS.
With reference to the country of origin markings on goods exported from
Japan, SCAP representatives stated that it appeared that a request for the omis-
sion of such markings on goods exported would only be approved in the event that
the goods were destined for a country which did not require such markings by vir-
tue of its internal legislation; and, that while it was recognized that goods
might be trans-shipped from such a country to another where the requirement ex-
isted, the laws of the latter should take care of the situation and that it was
impossible for SCAP to control any such trans-shipments.
NFTC COMMENT:
It is believed that goods exported from Japan should be marked with
Japan as the country of origin whether or not the laws of the importing country
require such markings.
ACCESS TO TECHNICAL INFORMATION:
With reference to the comments under the heading, "Access to Scientific
and Technical Information", SCAP representative stated that precaution is being
taken to insure that access is limited to information of Japanese right of origin
and ownership.
NFTC COMMENT.
Because of the vast amount of Japanese technical information originating
in the U. S. and the great difficulty of determining the source of such information,
the implementation of this policy is opposed in principle. In any event, there
should be no extension of the period for such access.and the utmost care should be
exercised in its administration.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose for the information of the Department
copies of correspondence between the Swiss Diplomatic Mission in Japan
A/EJH
and this Mission, in its capacity as the Diplomatic Sestion of General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, concerning a
proposed license agreement for the manufacture of DDT in Japan.
11
The Swiss Mission, in its note of June 30, 1947, requested
consideration of an enclosed license agreement proposed by J. R. Gigy,
Ltd., Basle, Switzerland, a Swiss firm said to be the owner of a
Japanese patent covering the manufacture of DDT insecticides, and
indicated that the Department had communicated with the Swiss Legation
at Washington concerning the matter in a note of November 4 1946.
(The Rnglish text of Japanese Patent No. 169665, received as enclosure
No. 4 to the Mission's note, is a lengthy document, dated November 21.
FILED
1942, describing the technical details of DDT production and chemical
reactions, and is not included as an enclosure to this despatch.)
21
This Mission replied by memorandum dated August 13, 1947, statf
that this Headquarters did not have authority to approve a license 
agreement of the type proposed. This reply was made at the request
of the Office of the Civil Property Custodian of this Headguarters,
which also stated that by such approval a national of a neutral nation
would be permitted to perform transactions forbidden to United Nations
D 
nationals, and that the lack of such an agreement did not impede the
production of DDT concentrate, which was already being carried on by
Japanese manufacturers.
3
The Swiss Mission, in a note of December 8, 1947, requested
reconsideration of the matter and enclosed a letter from the Far East
representative
63
14
RESTRICTED
ai

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
ESTRICTED
Tokyo's Despatch Mo. 700
October 30, 1948
representative of the Swiss firm, requesting information on the imports,
distribution and manufacture of DDr concentrates and insecticides in
4/
Japan. This Mission, in its reply by memorandum of August 3, 1948,
furnished information on the quantity of DDT concentrates imported
into Japan and the quantity of DDT products manufactured therefrom
from January 1, 1947, when the responsibility of accepting imports of
DDT concentrates and of storing and distributing DDT products was
transferred from the Occupation Forces to the Japanese Government,
to March 31, 1948, and informed the Mission thet the matter of
licensing Japanese manufacturers and the question of payment of
royalties must be held in abeyance for the present. This information
was furnished by the Public Health and Welfare Section and the Economic
and Scientific Section of General Headquarters, and the statement
relative to the proposed licensing agreement was based on the
recommendations of the Office of the Civil Property Custodian.
In accordance with a request of the Civil Property Custodian,
no reply was made to the statement in the enclosure to the Swiss
Mission's note of December 8, 1947 that royalties had not been paid
in the United States on DDT concentrates imported into Japan and that
manufacturing in Japan of DDT insecticides "is therefore also subject
to the agreement of the patent owner of Japanese Patent No. 169665".
This Mission has been informed by the Public Health and Welfare
Section that royalties on finished DDT products issued by the
Department of the Army are covered by separate agreements and that it
therefore appears that the Japanese Government is not responsible for
the payment of royalties on DDT imported before January 1, 1947, prior
to which date manufacturing was performed under procurement demands
issued by the United States Eighth Army, DDT concentrates being
furnished from United States Army stocks and finished products
returned to military depots for distribution.
The Office of the Civil Property Custodian has informed this
Mission that, as of this date, there has been no change in the policy
prohibiting such license agreements, that Japanese manufacturers are
using processes covered by patents held by other foreign owners, or
processes not registered in Japan, without the permission cr agreement
of the patent owners, and that in no case has the General Headquarters
approved a license agreement between Japanese manufacturers and a
foreign patent owner.
It is the opinion of this Mission that early consideration should
be given to the formulation of a policy regarding the use of foreign-
owned patents and processes, particularly in view of the recent
opening of the Japanese patent office to the registration of foreign
patents (this Mission's unclassified despatch No. 627 of September 23,
1948) and in view of provisions now being made for the payment of patent
royalties under pre-war license agreements.

MENORANDUM
DDT - manufacture in Japan - License agreement Geigy Patent Japan
No. 169665.
During August 1946, Messrs. Nichizui Trading Co. Ltd., Swiss owned fimm
at Osaka, acting as agents in Japan for Messrs. J. R. Geigy S.A., Basle
(Switserland), discovered infringements of Geigy's Japan Patent No. 169665
on the manufacture of DDT insecticides by Japanese manufacturers.
Japanese manufacturess were warned in November 1946 by Geigy's patent
Attorney. It then appeared from investigations, that SCAP's PHW Supply
Division-Production Branch had sponsored already in May 1946 Japanese
self-supply in conditioning DDT insecticides in order to reduce bulky
imports.
Conditioning started in various plants for 10% DDT dust and 5% DDT
liquid sprays which were produced with imported lo0% teohmical DDT
active ingredient. Later, when distribution and control of DDT
insecticides were handed to the Japanese Welfare Ministry, mamufacturer
began to produce also active DDT ingredient thus following SCaP's
suggestion to arrive at self-supply.
At present the Japanese Government plans production of approximately
300 metric tons and import from USA of approx. l00 metric tons of active
DDT ingredient per year to be conditioned to 10% DDT dust and/or 5% DDT
liquid spray as per requirement.
Messrs. J. R. Geigy S.A., Basle. Switzerland, who discovered DDT
insecticides in 1939 and who are holders of valid Patents in all
civilised countries, possess als: Patent Rights in Japan. Such rights
are covered with Japanese Patent No. 169665 issued on January 30th, 1945.
Japanese manufacturers of DDT insecticides, producing active DDT
ingredient or conditioning DDT insecticides number nine:
Nippon Soda Kabushiki Kaisha
Dainippon Seiyaku K. K.
Mitsubishi Kasei Kogyo K. K.
Ajinomoto Kaisha Tokyo
Teikoku Rokagaku Kogyo K. K.
Hodogaya Kagaku Kogyo K. K.
Kureba Spinning Mill
Mitui Kagaku Kogyo K. K.
Nippon Kayaku K. K.
Japanese Government officials of the Welfare Ministry had already in
October 1946 broached the question of purchasing the patent rights of
J. R. Geigy S.A. for the manufacture of DDT insecticides. Japanese
manufacturers too, knew of the rights of patentholder No. 169665.
However, commercial transactions and contracts between Japanese and
foreign individuals being subject to approcal of SCAP, negotiations for
license agreements for Patent No. 169665 were in abeyance until SCAP
granted clearance for entry into Japan of Geigy's Far Eastern
Representative, see note No. M.4.12.4-De of May 2nd, 1947 from the
Swiss Diplomatic Mission to SCAP's Diplomatic Section and their reply
of May
RESTRICTED

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.98)
RESTRICTER
Sub-Faclosure to Enclosure
No. 1 to Tokyo's Despatch No. 700
October 30, 1948
- 2
of May 23rd, 1947.
Since manufacture of DDT insecticides has been done in Japan on a
commercial basis, i.e., Japanese manufacturers sold DDT products in
respectable quantities to their Government and probably also in the
open market, those menufacturers are bound to pay royalties for the
patent rights of Messrs. J. R. Geigy S.A.
As it is the intention of SCAP to protect allied and others Patent
Rights it is requested that Geigy's rights are protected and manu-
facturers who produce DDT insecticides be held responsible to enter
license agreements with patentholder of Patent No. 169665, thus
enabling Geigy to obtain their rightful benefits from their invention.
Inventions which are covered by patents, represent intellectual property,
and coming from outside Japan, should be classified as imports. It is,
therefore, but logical that proceeds from them, in form of royalties, be
allowed to be remitted to the patentowner, either in hard cash or by
means which in no way will be to the disadvantage of the Patentee.
The Patentee should be entitled to realize the full value of his claimed
rights as it is enjoyed at the time of the transaction by the manufacturer
who makes use of the Patent. This is the practice in all civilised
countries adhering to the International Convention.
For the case at issue it is requested to have royalties to be paid
retroactively to the time when DDT insecticides manufacture started on
a commercial basis in Japan, i.e., May 1946. Such royalties in Jen be
permitted in free account and earmarked for transfer to Switserland.
Royalties forthcoming from the date of signature of SCap approved agree-
ments to be freely transferable in cash or in kind to Switserland.
The royalty to be five percent of the wholesale price of the finished
DDT insecticides, i.e., conditioned l0% DDT dust and 5% DDT sprays or
any concentration of DDT insecticides which is admitted for the
distribution to the public and as delivered by manufacturers ex their
works to official or public distributors.
The license on Patent No. 169665 is to be limited for the manufacture
of DDT insecticides for Japans domestic consumption only.
Licensees will be bound to keep proper records of manufacture and sale
and to periodical payments of royalties. The patentowner to be entitled
to control books and manufacturing plants of Licensees through Chartered
Accountants or the Patentee's own representative.
The duration of the license agreement to cover the same period as the
validity of the Patent, i.e., till 30th January 1960.
Differences of litigious character to be brought for decision to the
Arbitration Court of the International Chamber of Commerce only.
Tokyo, June 30th, 1947.
Far Eastern Representative
of
J. R. Geigy S. A. Basle.
To
B. Britt
SCAP - Public Health & Welfare Section
Supply Division - Production Branch
GHQ - Tokyo.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
MEMORANDUM
Tokyo, August 13, 1947.
Reference is made to memorandum No. F.9.3.2.3.-De of July 30, 1947
from the Swiss Diplomatic Mission, tranamitting four enclosures
regarding a proposed license agreement for the manufacture of DDT.
Under current Allied directives, this Headquarters does not
have requisite authority to approve a license agreement of the kind
envisaged and it is therefore regretted that this matter must remain
in abeyance until such time as license agreements between foreign
private individuals or firms and Japanese are practicable.
The four enclosures to the Mission's memorandum under
acknowledgment are returned herewith.

Memorandum
Geigy Patent Japan No. 169665.
SCAP sponsored manufacture of DDT insecticides in Japan with
imported DDT concentrate.
J. R. Geigy S.A., Basle, Switzerland, represented in Japan by
NICHIZUI TRADING CO. LTD., are holders of Japanese patent No. 169665
covering the manufacture of DDT insecticides.
Any kind of insecticide containing DDT active ingredient (DDT
concentrate-Dichloro-Diphenyl-Trichloraothane or related compounds)
is covered by the above mentioned patent.
Manufacture of DDT insecticide in Japan is subject to the agreement
of the owner of patent No. 169665. Such agreement provides for the
payment of royalties for patent rights.
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
Public Health and Welfare Section, Supply Division, first imported
into Japan DDT insecticides and later also DDT concentrate. Whilst
DDT insecticides imported by Public Health and Welfare Section, G.H.Q..
SCAP, Supply Division, from the United States of America were covered
by royalties in the country of manufacture and on the strength of
existing licence agreements, no royalties had been paid in the country
of origin (USA) on imported DDT concentrate. Manufacturing in Japan
of DDT insecticides with DDT concentrate imported by Public Health and
Welfare Section, G.H.Q., SCAP, or other sources is, therefore, also
subject to the agreement of the patent owner of Japanese patent
No.169665。
Manufacturers of DDT insecticides in Japan using imported DDT
concentrate, as for instance distributed by Public Health and Welfare
Section, Gm. SCAP, Supply Division, believe to be exempted from
obtaining agreement from the patent holder of Japanese patent
No. 169665 because Public Health and Welfare Section, G. H., SCaP,
had authorized as well as supervised such manufacture.
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
Public Health and Welfare Section, Supply Division Production Branch,
already during 1946 entrusted two Japanese manufacturers with the
production of DDT insecticides from DDT concentrate, namely:
Messrs. Ajinomoto Company Inc., Tokyo, and
Messrs. Teikoku Rikagaku Kogyo K.K., Magoya.
Imports of American DDT concentrate were made in sisable quantities
and the distribution program of DDT products as sponsored by Public
Health and Welfare Section, G. H. Q., SCAP, induced the Japanese Govern-
ment's Ministry of Welfare to appoint additional manufacturers of DDT
insecticides. The manufacturers not only produce DDT insecticides from
imported American but also from domestic DDT concentrate.
Reference is made to the memorandum attached to the note
F.9.3.2.3.-De of June 30th, 1947, of the Swiss Diplomatic Mission to

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
MEMORANDUM
Tokyo, August 3, 1948.
Reference is made to the Swiss Diplomatic Mission's notes
Nos. F.9.3.2.3.-De. of December 8, 1947 and F.9.3.2.3.-Dk. of
February 1l, 1948, requesting, on behalf of the Swiss firm
J. R. Geigy, Ltd., statistics on the import and manufacture of
DDT concentrates and insecticides in Japan and further requesting
reconsideration of a proposed licensing agreement for DDT production.
and to previous correspondence concerning this matter.
The Mission is advised that prior to January 1, 1947 all DDT
imported to Japan for civilian use was delivered to Occupation Forces
depots and distributed through Occupation Forces channels, with actual
release to the Japanese authorities accomplished at the prefectural
level under the supervision of the Military Government authorities.
At the time of release receipts were secured fron the responsible
Japanese officials to be used as a charge against the Japanese
Government.
The distribution of DDT through the Occupation Forces was
necessitated by the absence in the Japanese Government of an agency
qualified to carry out efficient distribution. However, on
January 1, 1947, the Japanese Government was charged wit h the
responsibility of accepting imports of DDT through the Japanese
Board of Trade and of storing and distributing DDT products required
for civilian uss.
With reference to the statement by J. R. Geigy, Ltd., in the
letter enclosed with the Mission's memorandum of December 8, 1947,
that the Public Health and Welfare Section of this Headquarters
entrusted Japanese manufacturers with the production of DDf during
1946, it should be stated that the manufacture of DDT products in
Japan prior to January 1, 1947 was performed under procurement demands
issued by the Commanding General, United States Eighth Army. DDT
concentrate was furnished from United States Army stocks and the
finished products were returned to military depots for distribution.
Although mamufacturing under procuremont demand was primarily for the
purpose of meeting civilian requirements, there was no segregation of
civilian and military stocks in military depots and the finished
products were available for military or civilian use as required.
According

Swiss Diplomatic Mission,
Tokyo.

to Tokyo's Despatch Ko. 700

Aocording to figures furnished this Headqmrters by the Japansse
Ministry of Welfare, during the period January 1, 1947 to March 31, 1948.
1.257,861 pounds of DDT concentrate valued at $814,339.21 were received,
and from this concentrate 6,228,866 pounds of 10% dusting powder and
1.241,835 gallons of 5 rosidual spray were produced.
The United States Eighth Army records of Japanese imports and
exports show that 1,042, 705 pounds of "Larvacide DDT Powder, dissolving"
were imported into Japan during l947 and none was imported during
January, February and March 1948. The difference between this figure
and the amount of concentrate reported as received by the Japanese
Ministry of Welfare may be accounted for by a possible misuse of
nomenclature for miscellaneous DDT products, considerable quantities
of which were imported during 1947.
With regard to the Mission's request for reconsideration of
the proposed licensing egreement, it is regretted that, inasmuch as
present policy prohibits transactions between persons in Japan and
persons outside of Japan involving patent property, the matter of
licensing Japanose manufacturers and the question of payment of
royalties must be held in abeyance for the present.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: NoV. 4, 1948
SUBJECT:
Japanese Production of Railroad Stokers
PARTICIPANTS:
r. Charles J. Surdy, Vice-Fresident, Standard
894.542/11-448
Stoker Company, New York City
Mr. Vernon, IR
COPIES TO:
E - Mr. Barnett
Mr. Edwards, FTC
ITP - Mr. Martin
lir. Gorman, Justice
1—1408
Mr. Surdy visited me at his request to state the following
case: A year ago, an official of SCAP wrote him requesting
a copy of his company's catalog relating to mechanized stokers
for locomotives. Recently, a Japanese minister in a public
statement indicated that the Japanese were installing a
stoker similar to the type manufactured by Mr. Surdy's
company in their coal-burning locomotives. Mr. Surdy said
that he wished to assure himself that his company received
adequate protection--protection in the sense of compensation
for production in Japan based upon his company's designs
and safeguards a ainst the possibility of competitive Japanese
C
exports to third couniry markets, particularly China. On
being asked whether any features of the stoker were patented,
Mr. Surdy stated that his company never bothered to take out
patents in Japan because they could not be effectively enforced.
He stated also that his company had agreements all over the
891.542/11-848
world--he mentioned in particular an agreement with a French
firm covering the continent of Europe--pursuant to which
companies were allotted territories for the manufacture and
sale of his stoker. The foreign firms, he said, pay his
company a royalty and receive both patent licenses and un-
patented information. On being asked whether the limitations
with respect to sales territories applied to unpatented as
well as patented products, Mr. surdy stated that the con-
tracts'did so provide.
I stated
1
2793

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
I stated to Mr. Surdy that the Department upon
request woula take every reasonable measure to protect
the interests of his company in Japan, China and else-
where, including assistance in assuring that his patent
rights were respected. I added, however, that the
Department did not consider that those rights extended
to unpatented technology and, indeed, that divisions
of markets based upon unpatented information were in
conflict with the Department's general policies.
Mr. Surdy evidenced a distinct understanding of this
fact and concluded with the observation that he
considered competition to be a good thing and necessary
to keep companies like his on their toes. He stated
that the general purpose of his meeting had been to
acquaint the Department with the situation in the
event that he should ask for assistance in the future.
ITP:IR:RVernon:osm
11-4-48

ENCLOSURE
POLICY TOWARD PATENTS, UTILITY MODELS AND DESIGNS IN JAPAN
As Forwarded by the Steering Committee
The Far Eastern Commission decides as a matter of policy
the following:
1. The Japanese Patent Office
The Japanese Fatent Office should continue to exercise
its functions, in such menner es will fulfill the previsions
in paragraphs 3, 4 and 5 below. The Japanese Patent Law should
contain explicit provisions requiring disclosure in the speci-
fications or drawings of all information necessary to the
working of a patented invention, and such provisions should be
strictly enforced. The Patent Cffice should resume the publi-
cation of officiel gazettes and petent specifications.
2. Patent Rights in Japan
No action should be taken to interfere with patent
rights in Japan which now exist, or which ere restored, or
which are newly ecquired during the period of occupation,
except
2. where the continued protection of such rights
is in conflict with policies established or to be established
in &ccordance with the terms of reference of the Far Eastern
Commission.
b. where such rights interfere with such produc-
tion, use, sale or importstion of products as are necessary to
carry out the purposes of the occupation. In such cases the
Supreme Commander should request the Japanese Government to in-
voke appropriate provisions of the Jepanese Patent Law to the
extent that such action may provide a remedy.
The provisions of this paragraph do not apply to
German-owned or former German-owned patents in Japan.
3. Secret Patents in Japen
The future meintenance in secrecy of any patent in
Japan should be prohibited, existing secret petents should be
published end treated in the seme manner as all other patents,
and pending applications heretofore treated as secret sheuld be
removed from such classification and treated in the same menner
as all other εpplications.
4. Patents in Japan Owned by Allied Nationals

netional is one whose property was treated as enemy property by
the Japanese Government, or whose property rights lapsed owing
to the existence of hostilities hetween Jpan and the country
of that mationel.

Japon. When the dete of entry into war is clearly inappliceble
to the case of a particular Allied national, as defined above,
the "effective date of loss" should be the actuel date of
sequestration of his petent, or the date on which his patent
rights lapsed).

SUBJECT: Japanese Patent Policy Proposals, FEC 284/10 and 311/2.
1. FEC 284/10.
The Soviet Delegation has objected to the inclusion of paragraph 5 of
this paper, and has recommended its deletion. It is our understanding that
the substance of the Soviet Delegation's objection goes to the reference in
the paragraph to members of "the Union for the Protection of Industrial
Property," an international convention to which the Soviet Union is not a
signatory. The Soviet Union has, however, agreed to the essential polioy
provisions of this paragraph in the Satellite Peace Treaties. To make
paragraph 5 entirely consistent with these peace treaties and attempt to
FW
remove the basis of Soviet opposition to FEC 284/10, you may therefore propose
the deletion of the words "which is a member of the Union for the Proteetion
of Industrial Property, starting in the secand line of paragraph 5, FEC 284/10,
8
and the substitution therefore of the words "at war with Japan."

Dear Mr. Saltzman:
Your attention is invited to our letter of October 13, and
your reply of November 5 concerning Japanese patents and trademarks.
894.542/12-2848
You may recall that enclosed with our October 13 letter was
a statement confirming several recommendutions presented verbally at a meeting
between representatives of the National Foreign Trade Council and representatives
of the Department of State in Washington on September 24, 1948. These same re-
commendations were reported to NFTC members in a bulletin; a copy of which is
attached. (NFTC Bulletin No. 1543). A representative (stationed in Japan) of
one of NFTC's member companies informally forwarded this last document to SCAP,
and on October 19 discussed the reco mendations set forth therein. In this
meeting, SCAP representatives raised a number of points on which further comment
was requested by NFTC.
The attached memorandum setting forth comments made by SCAP,
and NFTC replies thereto is submitted to you for the dual purpose of acquainting
your staff members interested in these problems, and enlisting your good offices
to see that our comments reach SCAP representatives through appropriate channels.
While it is highly probable that copies of this memorandum will reach SCAP on an
informal basis through one or more of our representatives in Japan, it is our
desire to adhere to our policy of dealing through the Department.
C
894.542/12-2848
You will recall that in several instances we have urged that
SCAP be permitted to act unilaterally in carrying out certain of our recommenda-
C
tions which might have the support of the Department. While the October 19 meet-
ing gave evidence of SCAP's keen interest in the recommendations submitted by NFTC,
it was made clear that further action must be based on directives from Washington.
In this connection your attention is invited to a statement of Mr. Frank McCoy on
December 9 concerning United States policy towards FEC 230. We would construe
Hr. McCoy's remarks to mean that SCAP has the power to issue instructions of its
own and without specific directions from Washington on measures designed to make
possible the early development of democratic and peacefully-inclined institutions
in Japan.
ONH

My dear Mr. Loree:
Reference is made to your letter of December 28,
1948, concerning the Japanese patent situation.
The Department thoroughly appreciates the need for
early action to restore normal patent relations between
Japan and other countries. To this end, the Depart-
ment has, as we have previously informed you, been
urging expedition of a policy decision by the Far Bastern
894.542/12-28
Commission. Owing to the complexity of the issues in-
volved, FEC action has taken longer than we had anti-
cipated, but the policy paper on the subject is now
in its final stages at the Commission.
SCAP is correct in stating that further action on
this matter must await a directive from Washington.
The parallel which you draw with General McCoy's state-
ment is incorrect in that the program to which he re-
ferred is being carried out by SCAP under a pre-existing
directive based upon a policy decision by the FEC and
not as a unilateral United States action.
2848
The memoranda which you attach setting forth comments
made by SCAP and the NFTC replies thereto are, in ac-
cordance with your request, being forwarded to SCAP. I
appreciate very much your also making these documents
available to the Department.

Japanese Patents and Trade-marks.
The National Foreign Trade Council has requested
that the attached documents be transmitted to SCAP for
its information. I would appreciate your transmitting
these documents to SCAP along with the information con-
tained in this memorandum.
We believe it should be made clear to SCAP, however,
that the Department of State does not underwrite the views
contained in these papers which are those of a purely
private organization. In a number of instances the Depart-
ment's policy relating to Japanese patents differs somewhat
from these recommendations.  It might be worthwhile to
point out, for instance, that with respect to the recommen-
dations for changes in the Japanese patent law, the Depart-
ment has consistently felt that the Japanese law is a
basically sound one, patterned on those of most European
countries. We do not feel that changes in the law should
be imposed upon the Japanese simply to bring the law in
line with standards of the American law.

Cable Address. NAPTRAC
Ref. No. M-1219
December 30, 1948
SUBJECT: PATENTS AND TRADEMARKS--JAPAN
1. What, if any, Japanese patent law is in effect at the present time:
Is Law No. 96, promulgated April 29, 1921, amended by Law No. 3 of
March 1937 the effective patent law?
2. Can applications for patents, with a power of attorney, be sent directly
to Japanese nationals for filing in Japan, or can such powers of attorney
be sent only to U. S. nationals?
3. What is the procedure for formalizing powers of attorney to be used in
connection with patent applications?
4. Is it required that powers of attorney, and other papers designating
agents for the purpose of obtaining and protecting patents be forwarded
to SCAP through the special Projects Division of the Department of State?
5. Is the Japanese Patent Office, at the present time, making examinations
of applications of patents as to novelty?
6. Approximately,how long after applications have been filed are patents
issued?
7. Can U. S. nationals enter into licensing agreements with Japanese nationals?
8. Are all patents which were cancelled by the Japanese War Legislation being
renewed?
9. Can trade marks which lapsed during the war be renewed?
10. Are provisions being made for the terms of all patents owned by United
Nations nationals and in force at the beginning of the war to be extended
for a period of 7 years?
ll. Are priority rights of United Nations nationals, which were in effect
December 7, 1941, to be extended for a period of 7 years?

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
SUBJECT: PATENTS AND TRADEMARKS JAPAN
Deoember 30, 1948
12. Will taxes on patents owned by U. S. nationals be waived for the period
from December 7, 1941 to December 1, 1948?
13. Will compulsory licensing, and other plans which have the effect of
diminishing the value of the right "to exclude" be eliminated from
Japanese patent law?
14. Are provisions being made to modify the Japanese patent law in the
following manner:
a. Amelioration of the unusually severe penalties.
b. Elimination of provisions for opposition proceedings.
c. Elimination of the requirement for payment of annual taxes.
d. Elimination of the provisions for working patents.

contemplated at the present time.

1948, with the recommendation that the application be favorably

DECLASSIFIED

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 8,
January 7, 1949.
authorization.
This Mission feels that to continue indefinitely to prohibit
foreign patent holders from entering into licensing agreements with
Japanese manufacturers, while permitting and encouraging the
Japanese use of such patents, unreasonably denies to foreign patent
owners the protection of their property rights to which they are
entitled. The officials concerned with the question, in the Office
of the Civil Property Custodian of this Headquarters, although
recognizing that this problem exists, have taken the position that
this Headquarters can take no remedial action, in regard to author
izing such licensing agreements, until directed to do so by the
Department of the Army.
In view of the trend toward the recognition of foreign patent
and copyright property rights, as evidenced by the opening of the
Japanese patent office to the registration of foreign patents and
authorization to Japanese publishers to enter into agreements for
publication rights for foreign books, this Mission suggests that
the Department may consider the present time appropriate to discuss
with the Department of the Army the question of directing this
Headquarters to permit licensing agreements between foreign patent
owners and Japanese manufacturers.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

1. Reference is made to the following:
a.
DS memo of 3 August 1948 to the Swiss Diplomatic Mission
(copy attached for info ESS/FC).
b.
ESS memo to the Danish Diplomatic Mission,
130(3 Aug 48)ESS/FC, subject, "Patent Royalties Due
Burmeister & Wain Co." (copy attached for info CPC).
c.
Memo of 23 November 1948 to Japanese Government, SCAPIN
6192-A, subject, "Use of United Nations Patents in the
Manufacture of VHF/FM Radio Equipment".
2. Reference "a" informed the Swiss Mission, on the basis of
CPC/PP check note of 31 January 1948, subject, "Swiss Mission's Request
for Information on DDT Production", that, inasmuch as present policy
prohibits transactions between persons in Japan and persons outside of
Japan involving patent property, the matter of licensing Japanese
manufacturers of DDT and the question of payment of royalties must be
held in abeyance for the present.
3. Reference "b" informed the Danish Mission that "present policy
governing foreign exchange transactions does not prohibit payment of
royalties for foreign patents used in Japan during the period of
occupation" and that the type of payment is dependent upon the terms of
the contract between the patent right holder and the user.
4. Reference "c" authorized certain listed Japanese manufacturers
of communications equipment to utilize American and British owned patents
in manufacturing Japanese police radio equipment, and established
procedures for the payment of royalties into the SCAP Custody Account
in the Bank of Japan.
5. There is now forwarded a copy of the Swiss Mission's note No.
F.9.3.2.3.-Du/e of 18 December 1948, requesting favorable consideration
of an inclosed application by the Nichizui Trading Co., Ltd. to collect
and preserve in a restricted account royalties accumulated and due for
the use of Japanese patent No. 169665, held by the Swiss firm J. R. Geigy
& Co., covering the manufadture of DDT insecticides, and mentioning as a
precedent
RESTRICTED

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclesure No. 2
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 8,
January 7, 1949
-2-
precedent the authorization given in reference "o".
6. The inclosed application states that the Japanese Ministry of
Welfare, Hinistry of Agriculture and Forestry, and respective manu-
facturers are willing to set aside reasonable royalties, equal to 5%
of the wholesale price of DDT insecticides manufactured, and are now
reserving such royalties. The Nichizui Trading Co., as agents for
J. R. Geigy & Co., proposes to place these accumulated royalties, and
future royalties monthly as they accumulate, in a restricted account
under the surveillance of the Japanese Ministry of Finance, and to
make withdrawals subject to the permission of the Ministry of Finance.
7. DS considers that, in view of Headquarters action in permitting
and encouraging the Japanese use of DDT processes without the agreement
of the patent owner, and in view of the undoubted value to the Occupation
of the products manufactured by those processes, every effort should be
made to permit the Geigy company to protect its interest in the matter.
Inasmuch as the payment of royalties for foreign patents is not
absolutely prohibited (Ref. "b"), and has in fact been authorised
without the existence of a licensing agreement in another case in which
manufacture was considered necessary in the interests of the Occupation
(Ref. "c"), DS considers the instant proposal in the absence of a
favorable decision by Headquarters permitting licensing agreements
between foreign patent holders and Japanese manufacturers, a reasonable
interim solution of this long-standing problem in connection with DDT
production.
8. DS therefore recommends that, inasmuch as royalties are already
being set aside by manufacturers, and the question of authorizing
licensing agreements is not involved, ESS and CPC take necessary action
to authorize the Nichizui Trading Co. to collect royalties and deposit
them in a restricted account, as requested.
9. DS further recommends that early consideration be given to
authorizing licensing agreements between Japanese manufacturers and
foreign patent holders.
