28 March 1949
PRESS RELEASE
FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
OFFICIAL PRESS RELEASE
One-Hundred Forty-fifth Meeting of the Far Eastern Commission
For Release 1n A.M. Newspapers, 30 March 1949
I. The Far Eastern Commission on 17 March 1949 approved a
policy decision relating to patents, utility models, and designs
in Japan. (text below)*
II. Pursuant to the Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern
Commission a directive based upon this policy decision has been
forwarded by the United States Government to the Supreme Comman-
der for the Allied Powers for implementation.
POLICY TOWARD PATENTS, UTILITY MODELS, AND DESIGNS IN JAPAN
The Fer Eastern Commission decides as a matter of policy
the following:
1. The Japanese Patent Office
The Japanese Patent Office should continue to exercise
its functions, in such manner as will fulfill the provisions in
paragraphs 3, 4 and 5 below. The Japanese Patent Law should con-
tain explicit provisions requiring disclosure in the specifica-
tions or drawings of all information necessary to the working of
a patented invention, and such provisions should be strictly en-
forced. The Patent Office should resume the puolication of
official gazettes and patent specifications.
2.
Patent Rights in Japan
No action should be taken to interfere with patent rights
in Japan which now exist, or which are restored, or which are
newly acquired during the period of occupation, except
a. where the continued protection of such rights is in
conflict with policies established or to be established in
accordance with the terms of reference of the Far Eastern
Commission;
b. where such rights interfere with such production,
use, sale or importation of products as are necessary to
carry out the purposes of the occupation. In such cases the
Supreme Commander should request the Japanese Government to
invoke appropriate provisions of the Japanese Patent Law to
the extent that such action may provide a remedy.
The provisions of this paragraph do not apply to German-
owned or former German-owned patents in Japan.
Press Release No. 52
-1-
145th FEC Mtg.

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
3. Secret Patents in Japan
The future maintenance in secrecy of any patent in Jepan
should be prohibited, existing secret patents should be published
and treated in the same manner as all other patents, and pending
applications heretofore treated as secret should be removed from
such classification and treated in the same manner as all other
applications.
4. Patents in Japan Owned by Allied Nationals
(For the purposes of this paragraph:
(1) An "Allied national" is one whose property was
treated as enemy property by the Japanese Government,
or whose property rights lapsed owing to the existence
of hostilities between Japan and the country of that
national.
(2) The "effective date of loss" applicable to the
patents or applications therefor of Allied nationals
should be the date of entry of the country of that
national into war with Japan. When the date of entry
into war is clearly inapplicable to the case of a
particular Allied national, as defined above, the
"effective date of loss" should be the actual date
of sequestration of his patent, or the date on which
his patent rights lapsed.)
a. Patents existing in Japan on the effective date of
loss or granted thereafter on applications filed before that
date, which were owned or had been applied for by Allied
nationals and which have since lapsed, or have been invali-
dated or sequestered or otherwise taken away from such
owners without their free consent should be subject to
revalidation and restoration upon request therefor made
by the Allied national owners within a reasonable period
and in a manner as may be provided by the Supreme Commander.
To such cases the following provisions should apply:
(1) Such owner should be afforded a reasonable time
within which to perform all legally reguired acts in
connection with restoration, revalidation, working and/or
maintenance of his rights which have not been performed
since the effective date of loss.
(2) The owner of a patent so restored or revalidated
shouid be entitled to all the rights and privileges to
which any national of any country is entitled under the
Jopanese patent law existing at the time of such restora-
tion.
(3) The owner to whom such patent is restored
shouid refund to the Japanese Government any sums
paid or credited to him that are the proceeds from
sale of the patent by the Japanese Government during
the war.
(4) Payment of any special or regular fees that
may have accrued to the Japanese Government with
respect to such petents from the effective date of
loss to date of such restoration should not be required.
b. Extension
At the request of the Allied national owner made
within & reasonable time (in such form and prior to such
date as may be prescribed by the Supreme Commander), the
Press Release No. 52

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.98)
period of duration of any Japenese patent owned by him on
the effective date of loss or granted thereafter on an ap-
plication filed before that date should be extended beyond
its normal expiration date. Such extension should be for a
period equal to that from the effective date of loss to the
effective date of restoration or to the normal expiration
dete of the patent, whichever is earlier, provided that such
owner waives all claim to royalties for use of the patent
from the effective date of loss to the date so fixed, and
remits any funds received by him or credited to him in a
blocked eccount as royelties for such use.
C.
Royelties
The Supreme Commander should not be responsible for
obtaining, or attempting to obtain, on behalf of Allied
nationals compensation for any use made of their patents
prior to date of restoration. However, the Supreme Commander
should make availeble to Allied national owners information
as tc funds credited to their accounts which constitute
royalties for uso of their rights from the effective date
of loss to date of restoration. If the Allied national owner
elects to receive such funds in lieu of an extension of term
as provided in para. b above, he should be entitled to do so
on thc same basis as other funds are restored to Allied
national property owners in Japen.
d. Applications Pending on Effective Date of Loss
Applicetions filed by Allied nationals for
patents in Japan which were pending on the effective date
of loss and on which patents were not issued should, upon
request of the Allied national applicant within a reasonable
time end in a manner prescribed by the Supreme Commander,
be reinstated as pending applications in the Japanese Patent
Office and be restorcd to the Allied national applicant.
Any patents thercafter issued on such applicetions should
be treated in a manner which conforms generally with
applicable provisions of subparagraph 4 a above.
5. Application to Registered Utility Models and Designs
This policy applies also to those patonts designated
as
"registered utility modols" and, to the extent epplicable,
to "registered designs".

145th FFC Mtg. - END.

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
A D D I T I O N A L I N F O R M A T I O N
(For use in connection with Press Release on Far Eastern
Commission Decision regarding Policy Toward Patents
Utility Models, and Designs in Japan)
For Release in A.M. Newspapers, 30 March 1949
The Far Eastern Commission at 1ts 145th meeting on 17 March
approved a decision setting forth the policy that will re-
establiah an effective patent system in Japan, assure full
publication of all patents in Japan, and provide for the restora-
tion of Allied-owned patent rights which were lost as a result
of the war.
This policy decision, the 56th approved by the Far Eastern
Comm ssion in its three years of continuous session in Washingtn,
falls logically into two parts: (1) general provisions govern-
ing all patents in Japan (paragraphs 1, 2, and 3 of the policy
decision); (2) special provisions applicable to Allied owners
of petent rights whose rights were lost during the war (para-
graph 4 of the policy decision).
GENERAL PROVISIONS
The Jepanese patent office, while already operating under
the guidance of the Occupat on authoritles, will now expand
its functions to approximately its pre-war standards, including
the publication of offic al gazettes and patent specifications
as in the past. Furthermore, under this policy decision the
Japanese patent law must contain explicit provisions requiring
the disclosure of specifications or drawings "of all information
necessary to the working of a patented invention." The policy
decision further reguires that those provisions "should be
strictly enforced."
In general, protection will be given under the policy to
all holders of patent rights in Japan, whether those rights
derive from patents now honored in Japan, from rights restored
under the policy, or fron rights newly acquired during the
period of occupation. However, certain qualifications are
placed upon the exercise of such rights. For example, the
protective provisions do not apply to German-owned or former
German-owned patents in Japan. Similarly, in any case where
the continued protection of patent rights conflicts with present
or future policies of the Far Eastern Commission, Commission
policies must be given precedence. Furthermore, in cases where
the continued protection of patent rights interferes with the
production, use, sale or importation of products "necessary to
carry out the purposes of the occupation," the usual protection
need not be granted. In such cases the Supreme Commander is
empowered to reguest the Japanese Government "to invoke appro-
priate provisions of the Japanese patent law" with a view to
protecting the interests of the Occupation. The Policy also
contains a provision preventing the maintenance in secrecy of
any patents in Japan, and requiring that existing secret patents
"should be published and treated in the same manner as ali other
patents." A further provision requires that pending applications
heretofore treated as secret "should be removed from such.classi-
fication and treated in the same manner as all other applications"
Press Release No. 52
- 1 -

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
SPECIAL PROVISIONS FOR ALLIED OWNERS OF PATENT RIGHTS
The new policy adopted by the Far Eastern Commission
contains special provisions whereby those Allied nationals
whose patent rights were lost during the course of the war
may remedy their situation and in some measure be compensated
for losses sustained. Under the policy any Allied nationals
who lost their normal patent rights may request, within a
reasonable period of time and in accordance with procedures
to be established by the Supreme Commander, the revalidation
and restoration of their patent rights. Upon restoration or
revalidation, such patent-holders are entitled to "all the
rights and privileges to which any national of any country is
entitled under the Japanese patent law existing at the time
of such restoration.
In requesting revalidation or restorat on, the Allied
patent-holder has a choice under the policy of either accepting
such funds as would normally have accrued to him during the
period in which he did not enjoy his full rights, or accepting
an extension of the duration of his patent for the same period
of time. Should he prefer to accept the extension of the
period of duration of his patent, he is required to remit any
funds received by him or credited to him from the effective
date of the loss of his rights to the date of restoration.
Similarly, he must waive all claim to royalties for use of
the patent during the period of loss. In this connection the
Supreme Commander, while not responsible under the policy for
attempting to obtain compensation for Allied nationals for
any use which may have been made of their patents during the
period of loss, is, however, responsible for making available
to such persons information regarding any funds credited to
their accounts in the form of royalties during that period.
A further provision in the new policy requires that
applications for patents in Japan which were filed by Allied
nat onals and which were pending on the effective date of loss
but cn which no patents were issued, should, upon request of
the original applicant, be reinstated as pending applications
in the Japanese patent office and restored to the original
applicant.
A final provision makes the policy applicable not only to
patents as generally understood but also to "registered utility
models" and "registered designs."
Summarizing, the patent policy adopted by the Far Eastern
Commission means the return to norinal operation of the Japanese
patent office, full publication of all patents in Japan, and
the restoration to Ailied nationals of patent rights lost
during the war.

: FE - Mr. John M. Allison

FROM : IR - Mr. Roger C. Dixon
suBject: Disposition of Japanese-owned patents butside Japan.
7ile
As you know, the proposed FEC "Policy on the Treat-
ment of Japanese Patents Outside Japan" (FEC 3ll Series)
was accepted by nine members of the FEC but not passed
because of the dissent of the Soviet delegate. Às a
result of this action, it was decided to seek agreement
on this subject outside the FEC. Before initiating inter-
governmental discussions, we presented the paper to the
OAP for its comments and concurrence. A number of sug-
gestions were made by the OAP to bring the paper in line
with the London Accord on German Patents Outside Germany.
The attached is a revision made in accordance with these
suggestions.
.ntral
If you have no objection, it is now proposed to call
5
an informal meeting of the members of Committee One of th
Document Must Be Retusuou Lo
FEC who were responsible for developing this policy and
whose governments approved the policy in the FEC. It is
8
felt that this will be the most expeditious way of handlin
94
the matter, since they are all familiar with the subject.
The purpose of the proposed meeting is to develop a pro-
cedure to be followed in obtaining international agreement
to the proposed plan for the disposition of Japanese patents
outside Japan. We have discussed the matter informally
with Mr. Thresher of the British delegation, who agrees
with this approach.
8
Assuming that agreement is reached among the FEC
countries attending the proposed meeting, it is our
249
intention to present the proposal to other governments
in order to achieve a very simple international accord on
Japanese patents outside Japan.

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.98)
Policy on the Treatment of Japanese Patants Outaide Janan
1. All former wholly Japanese-owned patents, isamed
country and in its possession or control under the general j
and regulations relating to Japanese-owned property, which
have not ceased or been dedicated to the public, should be
dedicated to the public or placed in the public domain es com-
tinuously offered for licensing without royalty except thet
these provisions should be subject to the right of each geverm-
ment to take appropriate measures to protect and preserve
proprietary, license, or other rights or interests, in such
patents which have been lawfully granted to or acquired by
any non-enemy national prior to June 30, 1949.
2. Unpublished applications for patents abandoned or
E
refused during the war should be published or otherwise made
available to the public.
3. All existing rights of non-enemy nationals in former
Japanese-owned patents should be protected.
4. The provisions of international conventions relating
to patents to which Japan was a party on the 6th December 1941,
should be applied to Japan, and applications for protection
should be entertained in respect of inventions made by Japanese
after the 1st January 1946, except for inventions relating to
articles, the manufacture, ownership or operation of which or
to processes, the ownership or operation of which, is forbidden
to the Japanese by policies established in accordance with the
Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission.
5. This policy applies also to those patents designated
as "registered utility models" and, to the extent applicable, to
"registered designs".

The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to refer to this
Mission's despatch no. 646 dated September 17, 1949, entitled
"Amendment to SCAPIN regarding Procedures for Restoration of Patents,
Utility Models, and Designs to Allied Nationals."
In this despatch mention was made of a Japanese Government
Cabinet Order issued to implement the terms of SCAPIN 1990, and the
supplements thereto, regarding procedures for restoration of patents,
utility models, and designs to Allied nationals. There is now en-
closed a copy of a translation of Cabinet Order no. 309, made
effective as from September 1, 1949, and entitled Cabinet Order
concerning Post-War Dispositions of Industrial Property Rights Owned
by Allied Nationals. This translation was prepared by Mr. K. YUASA,
a Japanese lawyer, and was issued under date of September 21, 1949,
by the American Chamber of Commerce in Japan, Tokyo, to members of
the Chamber.
While it is assumed that Mr. Yuasa's translation is reasonably
correct, this Mission wili expect to transmit to the Department a
copy of the official translation of this Cabinet Order which pre-
sumably will be published soon by the Japanese Government in the
English version of its Official Gazette.

894.542/10-749
The Secretary of State refers to despatck No. 699 dated
October 7, 1949 from the Acting United States Political Adviser
enclosing an unofficial English translation of Cabinet Order No. 309
concerning Postwar Dispositions of Industrial Property Rights Owned
by Allied Nationals.
Although the Acting Political Adviser states that an offieial
translation will be transmitted to the Department in the near future,
it is felt that on the basis of the unofficial translation, certain
discrepancies may exist between the Cabinet Order and the Policy
Towards Patents, Utility Models and Designs in Japan, promulgated
by the Far Eastern Commission in  arch, 1949.
The present definition of "Allied Nationals" in Artiele 2 of
the Order does not conform to the definition set forth in the Fac
Policy statement. The definition in the Order appears to give the
Minister of International Trade and Industry arbitrary power in
7
designating the persons to whom the Order shall apply.
Under Article 3, paragraph (1), line 9 of the Order, the worde
"at the time of lapse or cancellation" appear to be out of place.
Clerification. would be appreciated.
Article 8, paragraph (4) of the Order provides for payment of
certain patent fees in connection with resporation of patents. It is
not clear whether this provision is consistent with Paragraph 4 (4)
of the Patent Policy which provides that payment of fees from date of
loss to effective date of restoration should not be required.
Article 10 of the Order, providing for the rights of a third
party, states that such a person "may continue to use that patented
invention
47

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.97)
invention in the same form in whish he has been waing is.
the patent policy set forth in an interim direstive issued
and filed with the FEC, March, 1949, it is provided that
user shall be granted & non-exclusive liesnse. Sinee Arstel
does not specifically provide for the granting of a non-emala
license under the patented invention, it may be possible under
terms of this article to grant an exclusive license to a therd
party, inconsistent with the above directive.
Article 20 stipulates that the provisions of Artiale 9 to
Article l4 shall apply toutility models and designs. It is 
clear whether Article 1 through Article 8 also pertains to utilit
models and designs t the extent applicable,in accordance mith
paragraph five of the Fvc policy statement.
The Department feels that these inoonsistensies may be due
to translation difficulties and would appreciate any informatiom
which would clarify the matter.
A true sepy of

DRAFT CABLE TO SCAP
Reurad 2 26952 Oct. 22.
Re Part 1 Para (A). Applicability rEC patent policy not governed
by extent allied ownership. Test of policy's application is whether
or not patent was treated as enemy property by Japanese Government.
If patents in Japan of any companies organized under law of Japan were
permitted to lapse by government intervention in such companies because
of either direct or indirect enemy interest, patents should be restored.
re Part 1 Para(B). 1f patents treated as enemy property by
Japanese Government, provisions of FEC policy apply regardless of
expiration of patents during sequestration. Restoration will, of
course, be only for unexpired period of patent at time of seques-
tration or loss of rights.
re Part 2. rolicy principles re trademarks same as for patent
in rart 1, rara (A) above.

Sirs:
Your letter of Movember 9, 1949 to the Division of Seonomis
Property Poliey has been referred to this Office fer reply.
89
It is noted that your client, The Sperry Cerporation, is
94.542/11-949
interested in obtaining information as to the status of its patents
For your information and that of your client, there is atteehed a
copy of the poliey decision of the Far Eastern Commission on the sub-
ject of patents, utility models and designs in Japan. This poliey
provides that Allied nationals who lost their normal patent rights
during the course of the war may request the revalidation and rester-
ation oi their patent rights within a reasonable period of time and
in accerdance with procedures to be established by the Supreme Cem-
mander. The "reasonable period" for making applioations has beem
established by the Supreme Commander as being one year baginning
September 1, 1949.
Iou will also note that the policy provides that an Allied na-
tional may request extension of the period of duration of hie Japa-
nese patents for a period equivalent to that in whiah he did not
enjoy his full rights. If he accepts suah extension he is required,
however, to waive all claim to royalties for use of the patents dur-
ing the period of loss. While the Supreme Commander is not respon-
sible under the polioy for attempting to obtain compensation for
Allied nationals for any use which may have been made of their pat-
ents during the peried of loss, he is, hewever, responsible for mak-
ing available to suoh persons information regarding any funds credited
to their accounts in the form of royalties during that poriod.
With referenee to the paragraph which you quote from a letter of
Jamuary 13, 1949 to your client from Mr. Charles L. Hodge, Chief of
the United States Reparation and Restitution Delegation, polieies

and

In reply refer to
DS 894.542/11-1649
My dear Mr. Scarlett:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter dated
November 16, 1949, inquiring whether funds in the
amount of Yen 71,278.00 which the Northern Equipment
Con pany has on deposit in the Special Property Admini-
stration Account in Japan may be transferred into United
States dollars at the present time.
894.542/11-1649
Your designated agent in Japan may withdraw avail-
able funds from the Special Property Administration
Account upon the condition that an agreement be signed
at the time of withdrawal, whereby it is agreed that
any money withdrawn will be accounted for in the final
settlerent of the claim against the Japanese Government.
Funds obtained under this procedure may not be converted
into dollars or other foreign exchange at this time and
such money can not be transferred outside of Japan under
existing regulations.
The Japanese Government has been directed, however,
to preserve and protect the property of United Nations
nationals until such time as the property is restored
to the owner or his represontative.
Sincerely yours,
For the Secretary of State:
Hawley L. Smith

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
instructions Number 9 of February 1, 1949, transmitting
a letter from the Japanese Board of Patents for forward-
ing to the Yugoslav Patent Office, and Number 38 of
May 17, 1949, transmitting another communication from
the Japanese Board of Patents for forwarding to the
Yugoslav Patent Oice.
These communications were transmitted to the Yugoslav
Mihistry of Foreign Affairs under cover of Embassy motes
dated February 11 and May 31, 1949. On December 15, 1949,
the Embassy received a note from the Ministry of Foreign
Affairs in reply, advising that all information concern-
ing Yugoslav legislation on the subject of paterts and
property rights may be found in the official pu ication
of the International Bureau for the Protection of Industrial
Property entitled "Propriete Industrielle". The Ministry's
note further stated that the question of recognition of
Japanese patent and property rights in Yugoslavia was
closely related to the conclusion of a treaty of peace
with Japan.
The Department may wish to transmit this information
to the appropriate Japanese authorities.
Respectfully yours,
For the Charge d'Affaires, a.i.,
